Correlation coefficients between normal wiggly hesitant fuzzy sets and their applications
Authors: Qianzhe Wang, Minggong Wu, Dongwei Zhang, Peng Wang
Published: 2024

ABSTRACT
The multi-criteria decision-making (MCDM) field has long sought tools capable of adeptly capturing the intricacies of human decision-making amidst uncertainty. Hesitant fuzzy sets (HFS) have become a cornerstone in the MCDM field due to their ability to capture the intricacies of human decision-making under uncertainty. Nonetheless, we identified a significant gap in traditional HFS formulations, which often fail to fully harness the nuanced and implicit preferences of decision-makers (DMs). This shortcoming can lead to suboptimal decision outcomes in complex and uncertain environments. We introduce the normal wiggly hesitant fuzzy set (NWHFS), a novel construct that encapsulates both explicit and implicit preferences within a more representative framework. This study pioneers the development of new correlation coefficients for NWHFSs, offering a robust quantitative measure to elucidate the intricate relationships between variables. Our findings demonstrate that NWHFSs significantly enhance the MCDM process, providing a nuanced perspective that traditional HFS models cannot match. The proposed correlation coefficients not only reveal the concealed preferences of DMs but also broaden the decision-making spectrum, offering a more profound understanding of the relationships between alternatives and criteria. We illustrate the superiority of our approach through comparative analysis with existing methods, highlighting its ability to discern subtleties that other models overlook. Moreover, we integrate NWHFSs into clustering analysis, showcasing their potential to classify data sources with shared attributes effectively. This integration is particularly noteworthy for its ability to navigate complex datasets, offering a new dimension in data mining and resource retrieval. In essence, our study redefines the MCDM paradigm by introducing NWHFSs and their correlation coefficients, setting a new standard for decision-making accuracy and insight. The implications of our work extend beyond theory, offering practical solutions to real-world decision-making challenges.

Introduction

In the current milieu of decision-making, the accurate portrayal of decision-makers’ (DMs’) evaluations is paramount. Hesitant fuzzy sets (HFS) have risen as a prominent methodology for this purpose1,2. The HFS framework has been extensively applied across various domains, including multi-criteria decision-making (MCDM)3–5and clustering analysis, among others. As the application spectrum of HFS has broadened, a growing number of scholars have dedicated efforts to enhance these sets. Consequently, numerous extensions of HFS have been proposed to address practical issues with greater precision. For instance, dual hesitant fuzzy sets (DHFSs)6–11, which consider both membership and non-membership degrees simultaneously, have been developed to integrate HFS with intuitionistic fuzzy sets (IFS). Other extensions such as hesitant fuzzy linguistic terms set (HFLTS)12–15, interval-valued hesitant fuzzy set (IVHFS)16–18, probabilistic hesitant fuzzy set (P-HFS)19–21, and hesitant fuzzy soft set (HFSS)22,23 have also been introduced, enriching the body of knowledge surrounding HFS.

A pivotal aspect of HFS theory is the correlation coefficient, which reflects the relationship between two variables24. Initially, correlation coefficients between hesitant fuzzy elements (HFEs) were proposed, and later, these were extended to HFSs25. Despite these advancements, there are limitations. Both methodologies necessitate a specific complementing strategy to ensure the lengths of two HFEs are aligned. Moreover, the deduced correlation coefficients are invariably positive, which deviates from traditional statistical norms. To address these shortcomings, novel mean and variance concepts for HFSs were introduced26, along with new correlation coefficients. Subsequent works27 and28 highlighted that the approach presented by26 overlooked the length of HFEs in HFSs, leading to the derivation of several synthetic correlation coefficients. In another vein, generalized forms25 were extended by introducing parameters29.

It is noteworthy that existing correlation measures continue to focus on the original evaluation information provided by DMs, often neglecting the hidden preferences that can significantly influence the linear relationships between variables. Beyond the correlation coefficients for HFSs, scholars have also concentrated on those for the aforementioned HFS extensions. For example, a correlation measuring method between HFLTSs was proposed30, and research on correlation coefficients for IVHFSs, P-HFSs, and HFSSs has been conducted31–33. These studies have not only enriched HFS-related theories but also found broad applications in decision-making33,34 and clustering analysis25,27.

The decision-making process facilitated by the use of a correlation coefficient aims to ascertain the relationships between alternatives and a reference set. Clustering analysis, on the other hand, strives to group objects with higher similarity based on data characteristics, serving as a powerful tool for data mining35 and resource retrieval36. While these extended correlation coefficients have become more comprehensive, their complex forms might lead DMs to focus on detailed object descriptions, potentially overlooking the expression of their own preferences.

Drawing inspiration from the triangular hesitant fuzzy set (THFS)37 and interval-valued hesitant fuzzy set (IVHFS)38, a novel form of hesitant fuzzy set (HFS) known as the normal wiggly hesitant fuzzy set (NWHFS) has been introduced39. This innovative approach introduces a mechanism to address the concealed uncertain preferences of decision-makers (DMs). The term ’concealed uncertain preferences’ encapsulates the implicit preferences that DMs may not explicitly articulate but that significantly influence their decision-making process. For example, when evaluating a project, DMs might subconsciously favor options that align with their organization’s long-term goals, even if such alignment is not directly expressed. Another example, in environmental quality evaluation39, the inability to differentiate between closely aligned policy options can result in the selection of a less effective strategy.In addressing this research gap, our study introduces the Normal Wiggly Hesitant Fuzzy Set (NWHFS), a novel construct that not only acknowledges the explicit evaluations provided by DMs but also delves into the unspoken, implicit preferences that significantly influence decision-making.

Within the diverse applications of HFS, MCDM models stand out as particularly significant3. They serve as essential tools for addressing complex decision-making scenarios characterized by a multitude of criteria and options, often competing against each other-a common challenge in real-world contexts. While traditional MCDM models are indeed valuable, they sometimes fall short in capturing the subtle preferences and hesitations inherent to decision-makers.

Herein lies the utility of the novel approach afforded by NWHFS-based MCDM methods. These methods leverage the rich representational capacity of NWHFS to enhance the decision-making process. They provide a granular and realistic portrayal of the decision-making landscape and establish a robust framework for evaluating and comparing alternatives across a range of criteria. Such models are indispensable for formulating decisions that are not only well-informed but also capable of withstanding the complexities and uncertainties of the real world.

These NWHFS-based MCDM methods contribute a sophisticated and nuanced framework for decision analysis. They facilitate a more precise reflection of the decision-makers’ genuine preferences and the uncertainties they face, thereby enabling the attainment of more dependable and robust decision outcomes.

In summary, the evolution of Hesitant Fuzzy Sets (HFS) has seen a proliferation of complex extensions, each attempting to capture the multifaceted nature of uncertainty in decision-making. However, this complexity has inadvertently drawn attention away from the essence of decision content, prompting a need for a more focused and content-driven approach. The introduction of Normal Wiggly Hesitant Fuzzy Sets (NWHFS) is a response to this need, steering decision-makers (DMs) to concentrate on the intrinsic value of their evaluations rather than being preoccupied with formalistic representations. Despite the advancements in HFS and its extensions, a significant research gap emerges when it comes to the direct application of existing methodologies for measuring correlations within NWHFSs. The current methods, primarily tailored for original evaluation information, have not been designed to explore the nuanced relationships encapsulated within NWHFSs. This gap underscores the necessity for the development of novel correlation coefficients specifically crafted for NWHFSs, which can accurately reflect the complex interplay of decision elements.This paper takes a pivotal step in filling this research void by introducing new correlation coefficients for NWHFSs, which are not only defined but also contextualized through their weighted forms. Drawing inspiration from seminal works25–28, we extend the frontiers of HFS theory by deriving these coefficients and validating their feasibility through comparative analyses with existing models25,26. Our approach not only addresses the limitations of previous methods but also introduces a layer of sophistication that aligns with the intricate dynamics of real-world decision scenarios.

The remainder of this paper is organized as follows: “Preliminaries” reviews the basic concepts of HFS, along with the correlation coefficients proposed by previous scholars25,26 and special examples. In addition to this introduction, we recount the concepts of normal wiggly hesitant fuzzy sets and compare the differences in form and meaning between HFS and NWHFS. In “Correlation coefficients between NWHFSs”, we propose correlation coefficients and their weighted forms between NWHFSs and evaluate their superiority with examples. Section “Typical applications and comparisons” presents two typical examples showing the application of the novel correlation coefficients between NWHFSs in MCDM processes and clustering analysis. Finally, “Conclusion” concludes the paper. The flowchart of our entire algorithm is shown in Fig. 1.Figure 1Flowchart of the algorithm in this paper.

Flowchart of the algorithm in this paper.

Preliminaries

In this section, we will review basic concepts of HFSs, as well as their correlation coefficients proposed by25 and26. By analyzing special examples, we evaluate the drawbacks of existing calculating methods in detail. We will recount some concepts of NWHFSs and distinguish the differences between NWHFS and HFS.

Hesitant fuzzy sets

The theoretical tapestry of fuzzy logic is enriched by the concept of HFS, which have emerged as a sophisticated tool to model the complexities of human decision-making in the presence of uncertainty and ambiguity.We will take an in-depth look at HFS to provide a solid foundation for the subsequent study of the nuances of NWHFS.

Definition 1

1 The cornerstone of HFS is the hesitant fuzzy element (HFE), which encapsulates the inherent hesitancy of decision-makers (DMs) in pinning down a single membership degree. Mathematically, Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X=\left\{ x_{1}, x_{2},\right.$$\end{document}X=x1,x2, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left. \ldots , x_{n}\right\}$$\end{document}…,xn denote a reference set. Then the hesitant fuzzy set A on X return a subject of [0,1] can be defined as241\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} A=\left\{ \left\langle x, h_{A}(x)\right\rangle | x \in X\right\} \text {,} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}A=x,hA(x)|x∈X,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_{A}(x)$$\end{document}hA(x) represents a set of possible membership degrees, reflecting the DM’s hesitancy.

HFS transcend the conventional fuzzy set paradigm by acknowledging the cognitive complexities in decision-making. This theoretical advancement is essential for scenarios where DMs grapple with multiple plausible membership degrees, thus providing a more authentic representation of the decision-making milieu.The utility of HFS has been demonstrated across a spectrum of disciplines, including decision analysis, risk assessment, and policy-making. Their capacity to model the subtleties of decision-making under uncertainty has been instrumental in enhancing the robustness and reliability of decision outcomes.

Correlation coefficients of HFSs

Many researchers devoted themselves to the measuring of the correlation coefficients between HFSs.24 proposed several correlation coefficients between HFEs.25 and26 extended correlation coefficients between HFSs with the consideration of energy information and stochastic process rules, respectively. As per our knowledge, there still are some limitations for the correlation formulas in special examples. We will illustrate the drawbacks of aforementioned researches along with several examples.

{\overline{B}} ]}^2}} } \right\} }^{1/2}}}}. \end{array} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ρHFS2=∑i=1n[h¯A(xi)-A¯]·[h¯B(xi)-B¯]∑i=1n[h¯A(xi)-A¯]21/2·∑i=1n[h¯B(xi)-B¯]21/2.

Example 2

Let A, B and C are three HFSs in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X=\left\{ x_{1}, x_{2}\right\}$$\end{document}X=x1,x2, and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\begin{array}{l} A\mathrm{{ = }}\left\{ {\left\langle {{x_1},(0.3,0.5)} \right\rangle ,\left\langle {{x_2},(0.3,0.6,0.9)} \right\rangle } \right\} , \\ B\mathrm{{ = }}\left\{ {\left\langle {{x_1},(0.2,0.6)} \right\rangle ,\left\langle {{x_2},(0.2,0.7,0.9)} \right\rangle } \right\} , \\ C\mathrm{{ = }}\left\{ {\left\langle {{x_1},(0.2,0.3,0.7)} \right\rangle ,\left\langle {{x_2},(0.5,0.7)} \right\rangle } \right\} . \end{array} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}A=x1,(0.3,0.5),x2,(0.3,0.6,0.9),B=x1,(0.2,0.6),x2,(0.2,0.7,0.9),C=x1,(0.2,0.3,0.7),x2,(0.5,0.7).According to Eq. (5), we can calculate the mean of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_{A}(x)$$\end{document}hA(x), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_{B}(x)$$\end{document}hB(x) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_{C}(x)$$\end{document}hC(x) as following:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\begin{array}{l} {{\overline{h}} _A}(x) = {{\overline{h}} _B}(x) = {{\overline{h}} _C}(x) = \{ 0.4,0.6\} \end{array}\end{aligned}$$\end{document}h¯A(x)=h¯B(x)=h¯C(x)={0.4,0.6}And we can get the mean of HFSs A, B and C:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\begin{array}{l} {\overline{A}} = {\overline{B}} = {\overline{C}} = 0.5, \end{array}\end{aligned}$$\end{document}A¯=B¯=C¯=0.5,Per26’s methodology, the correlation coefficients between A and B, A and C, B and C can be obtained as following:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\begin{array}{l} {\rho _{HFS2}}(A,B) = {\rho _{HFS2}}(A,C) = {\rho _{HFS2}}(B,C) = 1. \end{array} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ρHFS2(A,B)=ρHFS2(A,C)=ρHFS2(B,C)=1.

26 has highlighted the shortcomings of the approach presented by25. However, in certain special cases, the method proposed by26 also fails to distinguish between the correlation coefficients of Hesitant Fuzzy Sets (HFSs). For instance, even though sets A, B, and C are distinctly different from one another, the correlation coefficients calculated by26’s method yield identical results. The methodology of26 has been deemed insufficient by27, who argue that it considers only the mean values and thus neglects other critical aspects.27, on the other hand, advocate for a more holistic approach that takes into account the length, variance, and mean values of Hesitant Fuzzy Elements (HFEs) concurrently. In this work, we have dedicated ourselves to developing an alternative solution, which involves the computation of correlation coefficients that consider both the deeper layers of uncertainty and the original HFSs.

Despite their widespread utility, traditional HFS models have encountered limitations in capturing the full spectrum of DMs’ preferences. This has catalyzed the evolution towards more expressive forms, such as NWHFS, which offer a richer and more nuanced representation of decision-making processes.

Normal wiggly hesitant fuzzy sets

Recently,39 introduced a novel generalization of Hesitant Fuzzy Sets (HFS), termed Normal Wiggly Hesitant Fuzzy Sets (NWHFS). This innovative formulation is adept at capturing the more profound and uncertain preferences of decision-makers (DMs). The NWHFS extension retains the beneficial attributes of HFS while presenting an enhanced suitability for addressing practical challenges. In the following subsection, we delve into the fundamental concepts of Normal Wiggly Hesitant Fuzzy Sets.

Definition 4

39 A Normal Wiggly Hesitant Fuzzy Set (NWHFS) on a reference set X is defined as a collection of normal wiggly hesitant fuzzy elements (NWHFEs), each representing a more nuanced and comprehensive expression of uncertainty in decision-making. Mathematically, it is represented as:6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{array}{l} NW = \left\{ {\left\langle {x,h(x),\psi (h(x))} \right\rangle \left| {x \in X} \right. } \right\} \text {,} \end{array} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}NW=x,h(x),ψ(h(x))x∈X,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h(x)=\left\{ \gamma _{1}, \gamma _{2}, \ldots , \gamma _{\# h}\right\}$$\end{document}h(x)=γ1,γ2,…,γ#h called HFE, which can be abbreviated to h, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\# h$$\end{document}#h denoted as the number of values in HFE. The function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi (h(x))$$\end{document}ψ(h(x)) is termed as a Normal Wiggly Element (NWE), encapsulating the subtleties of decision-makers’ preferences. The NWE, denoted as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi (h(x))$$\end{document}ψ(h(x)) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$= \left\{ {\mathop {\gamma _{1}}\limits ^{\smile }}, {\mathop {\gamma _{2}}\limits ^{\smile }}, \cdots {\mathop {\gamma _{\# h}}\limits ^{\smile }} \right\} {\mathop {\gamma _{i}}\limits ^{\smile }} = \{ {a_i}^L,{a_i}^M,{a_i}^U\},$$\end{document}=γ1⌣,γ2⌣,⋯γ#h⌣γi⌣={aiL,aiM,aiU}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i = 1,2, \ldots ,\# h$$\end{document}i=1,2,…,#h are the values in NWE, symbolizing the lower, middle, and upper bounds of the preference degree, respectively. The range of each value in NWE can be defined as:7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}{} & {} \{ {a_i}^L,{a_i}^M,{a_i}^U\} \nonumber \\{} & {} \quad = \left\{ \begin{array}{l} \max \left( {({\gamma _i} - g({\gamma _i})),0} \right) ,\\ \left( {2rpd(h(x)) - 1} \right) g({\gamma _i}) + {\gamma _i},\\ \min \left( {({\gamma _i} + g({\gamma _i})),1} \right) \end{array} \right\} , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}{aiL,aiM,aiU}=max(γi-g(γi)),0,2rpd(h(x))-1g(γi)+γi,min(γi+g(γi)),1,The wiggly function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g\left( \gamma _{i}\right)$$\end{document}gγi is introduced to modulate the membership degrees, reflecting the dynamic nature of preferences. It is defined as:8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{array}{l} g({\gamma _i}) = \sigma \cdot {e^{ - \frac{{{\gamma _i} - {{\bar{h}}}}}{{2{\sigma ^2}}}}}\text {,} \end{array} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}g(γi)=σ·e-γi-h¯2σ2,where the mean and variance of HFE are defined as following, respectively.9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}{} & {} \begin{array}{l} {\overline{h}} = \frac{1}{{\# h}}\sum \limits _{i = 1}^{\# h} {{\gamma _i}}\text {,} \end{array} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}h¯=1#h∑i=1#hγi,10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}{} & {} \begin{array}{l} \sigma = \sqrt{\frac{1}{{\# h}}\sum \limits _{i = 1}^{\# h} {{{({\gamma _i} - {\overline{h}} )}^2}} }\text {.} \end{array} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}σ=1#h∑i=1#h(γi-h¯)2.Real Preference Degree (RPD)39: A pivotal aspect of NWHFS is the real preference degree, rpd(h) which quantifies the inclination of decision-makers towards certain membership degrees. It is calculated as follows:11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{array}{l} {\mathop {\text {rpd}}\nolimits } (h) = \left\{ {\begin{array}{*{20}{c}} {\sum \limits _{i = 1}^{\# h} {{{{\tilde{\gamma }} }_i}} \left( {\frac{{\# h - i}}{{\# h - 1}}} \right) ,}&{}{\mathrm{{ if }}{\overline{h}} < 0.5}\\ {1 - \sum \limits _{i = 1}^{\# h} {{{{\tilde{\gamma }} }_i}} \left( {\frac{{\# h - i}}{{\# h - 1}}} \right) ,}&{}{\mathrm{{ if }}{\overline{h}} > 0.5}\\ {0.5,}&{}{\mathrm{{ if }}{\overline{h}} = 0.5} \end{array}} \right. \text {,} \end{array} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}rpd(h)=∑i=1#hγ~i#h-i#h-1,ifh¯<0.51-∑i=1#hγ~i#h-i#h-1,ifh¯>0.50.5,ifh¯=0.5,where the normalized form of values in HFE is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\gamma }_{i}=\gamma _{i} / \left( \sum _{i=1}^{\# h} \gamma _{i}\right)$$\end{document}γ~i=γi/∑i=1#hγi. Therefore,39 called the pairwise \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle h(x), \psi (h(x))\rangle$$\end{document}⟨h(x),ψ(h(x))⟩ as normal wiggly hesitant fuzzy element (NWHFE), which can be abbreviated to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle h, \psi (h)\rangle$$\end{document}⟨h,ψ(h)⟩.

This refinement of the NWHFS concept is essential for understanding the subsequent development of our study, particularly in the formulation of new correlation coefficients and their application in decision-making scenarios.

Definition 5

39 For a NWHFS in reference set X, the mean of NWE can be denoted as12\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{array}{l} {\overline{\psi }} (h(x)) = \frac{1}{{\# h}}\sum \limits _{i = 1}^{\# h} {\overline{\mathop {\gamma _{i}}\limits ^{\smile }}}\text {,} \end{array} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ψ¯(h(x))=1#h∑i=1#hγi⌣¯,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\overline{\mathop {\gamma _{i}}\limits ^{\smile }}} = \frac{{{a_i}^L + {a_i}^M + {a_i}^U}}{3}$$\end{document}γi⌣¯=aiL+aiM+aiU3.

Example 3

Suppose F is a HFS in reference set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X=\left\{ x_{1}, x_{2}\right\}$$\end{document}X=x1,x2 , and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\begin{array}{l} F\mathrm{{ = }}\left\{ {\left\langle {{x_1},(0.2,0.4)} \right\rangle ,\left\langle {{x_2},(0.5,0.6,0.9)} \right\rangle } \right\} \text {.} \end{array}\end{aligned}$$\end{document}F=x1,(0.2,0.4),x2,(0.5,0.6,0.9).According to Definition 4, we can get NWHFS\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\begin{array}{l} N{W_F}\mathrm{{ = }}\left\{ \begin{array}{l} \left\langle \begin{array}{l} {x_1},(0.2,0.4),\{ (0.1393,0.1798,\\ 0.2607),(0.3393,0.3798,0.4607)\} \end{array} \right\rangle ,\\ \left\langle \begin{array}{l} {x_2},(0.5,0.6,0.9),\{ (0.3949,\\ 0.5210,0.6051),(0.4426,0.6315,\\ 0.7574),(0.8338,0.9132,0.9662)\} \end{array} \right\rangle \end{array} \right\} \text {.} \end{array}\end{aligned}$$\end{document}NWF=x1,(0.2,0.4),{(0.1393,0.1798,0.2607),(0.3393,0.3798,0.4607)},x2,(0.5,0.6,0.9),{(0.3949,0.5210,0.6051),(0.4426,0.6315,0.7574),(0.8338,0.9132,0.9662)}.

To address the challenge of uncertainty where decision-makers (DMs) are unable to pinpoint a definitive value, Hesitant Fuzzy Sets (HFS) allow for the retention of multiple uncertain values within a single set. Normal Wiggly Hesitant Fuzzy Sets (NWHFS), as proposed by39, offer a more profound revelation of information through the function of real preference degrees. The outcomes of decisions are anchored in the original information provided by DMs, enriched by the potential preference degrees articulated in the aforementioned Definition 4. Normal Wiggly Elements (NWEs) encapsulate valuable, concealed information derived from the original Hesitant Fuzzy Elements (HFEs). As depicted in Example 3, each NWE is associated with a triangular representation, where the positioning of the triangle’s vertices symbolizes the subjective, potential preferences of the DMs. These distinctions in the structure and significance between HFS and NWHFS have prompted our exploration of novel correlation coefficients designed to elucidate the relationships between NWHFSs.

Correlation coefficients between NWHFSs

Considering the implicit preferences of decision-makers (DMs) is essential in certain decision-making problems where subjective inclination significantly influences the outcome. The correlation coefficient serves as a valuable tool for gauging the extent of linear correlation between two variables. Consequently, it is both pertinent and practical to delve into the correlation coefficients of Normal Wiggly Hesitant Fuzzy Sets (NWHFSs) to elucidate the distinctive relationships between evaluated objects and the reference candidate set. In this section, we introduce the concept of correlation coefficients between NWHFSs, explore their properties, and demonstrate the merits of our newly proposed correlation coefficients through illustrative examples.

Definition 6

For a reference set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X=\left\{ x_{1}, x_{2}, \ldots ,\right.$$\end{document}X=x1,x2,…, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left. x_{n}\right\}$$\end{document}xn, \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A=\left\{ \left\langle x, h_{A}\left( x_{i}\right) , \psi \left( h_{A}\left( x_{i}\right) \right) \right\rangle | x_{i} \in X\right\}$$\end{document}A=x,hAxi,ψhAxi|xi∈X and B \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_{B}\left( x_{i}\right) =\left\{ \gamma _{B i 1},\gamma _{B i 2}, \ldots , \gamma _{B i \# h_{B i}}\right\}$$\end{document}hBxi=γBi1,γBi2,…,γBi#hBi {\overline{\psi }} ({h_A}({x_i}))} \right) \cdot \left( {{{{\overline{h}} }_B}({x_i}) - {\overline{\psi }} ({h_B}({x_i}))} \right) } \right] } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}C(A,B)=1n∑i=1nh¯A(xi)-ψ¯(hA(xi))·h¯B(xi)-ψ¯(hB(xi))where,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}{} & {} \begin{aligned} {{\overline{h}} _A}({x_i}) = \frac{1}{{\# {h_{Ai}}}}\sum \limits _{k = 1}^{\# {h_{Ai}}} {{\gamma _{Aik}}}, \end{aligned} \\{} & {} \begin{aligned} {{\overline{h}} _B}({x_i}) = \frac{1}{{\# {h_{Bi}}}}\sum \limits _{l = 1}^{\# {h_{Bi}}} {{\gamma _{Bil}}}, \end{aligned} \\{} & {} \begin{aligned} {\overline{\psi }} ({h_A}({x_i})&= \frac{1}{{\# {h_{Ai}}}}\sum \limits _{k = 1}^{\# {h_{Ai}}} {\overline{\mathop {\gamma _{Aik}}\limits ^{\smile }}} \\&= \frac{1}{{\# {h_{Ai}}}}\sum \limits _{k = 1}^{\# {h_{Ai}}} {\frac{{{a_{Aik}}^L + {a_{Aik}}^M + {a_{Aik}}^U}}{3}}, \end{aligned} \\{} & {} \begin{aligned} {\overline{\psi }} ({h_B}({x_i})&= \frac{1}{{\# {h_{Bi}}}}\sum \limits _{l = 1}^{\# {h_{Bi}}} {\overline{\mathop {\gamma _{Bil}}\limits ^{\smile }}} \\&= \frac{1}{{\# {h_{Bi}}}}\sum \limits _{l = 1}^{\# {h_{Bi}}} {\frac{{{a_{Bil}}^L + {a_{Bil}}^M + {a_{Bil}}^U}}{3}}. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}h¯A(xi)=1#hAi∑k=1#hAiγAik,h¯B(xi)=1#hBi∑l=1#hBiγBil,ψ¯(hA(xi)=1#hAi∑k=1#hAiγAik⌣¯=1#hAi∑k=1#hAiaAikL+aAikM+aAikU3,ψ¯(hB(xi)=1#hBi∑l=1#hBiγBil⌣¯=1#hBi∑l=1#hBiaBilL+aBilM+aBilU3.Therefore, we can easily get C(A, A) as following:14\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{array}{l} C(A,A) = \frac{1}{n}{\sum \limits _{i = 1}^n {\left[ {{{{\overline{h}} }_A}({x_i}) - {\overline{\psi }} ({h_A}({x_i}))} \right] } ^2}\\ = \frac{1}{n}{\sum \limits _{i = 1}^n {\left[ {\frac{{\sum \limits _{k = 1}^{\# {h_{Ai}}} {{\gamma _{Aik}}} }}{{\# {h_{Ai}}}} - \frac{{\sum \limits _{k = 1}^{\# {h_{Ai}}} {\overline{\mathop {\gamma _{Aik}}\limits ^{\smile }}}}}{{\# {h_{Ai}}}}} \right] } ^2}\\ = \frac{1}{n}{\sum \limits _{i = 1}^n {\left[ {\frac{{\sum \limits _{k = 1}^{\# {h_{Ai}}} {{\gamma _{Aik}}} }}{{\# {h_{Ai}}}} - \frac{{\sum \limits _{k = 1}^{\# {h_{Ai}}} {\frac{{{a_{Aik}}^L + {a_{Aik}}^M + {a_{Aik}}^U}}{3}} }}{{\# {h_{Ai}}}}} \right] } ^2}. \end{array} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}C(A,A)=1n∑i=1nh¯A(xi)-ψ¯(hA(xi))2=1n∑i=1n∑k=1#hAiγAik#hAi-∑k=1#hAiγAik⌣¯#hAi2=1n∑i=1n∑k=1#hAiγAik#hAi-∑k=1#hAiaAikL+aAikM+aAikU3#hAi2.

Theorem 1

The correlation coefficient formula given as Eq. (15) between any NWHFSs satisfies the following properties:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\rho _{NW1}}(A,B) = {\rho _{NW1}}(B,A)$$\end{document}ρNW1(A,B)=ρNW1(B,A) ;\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\rho _{NW1}}(A,A) = 1$$\end{document}ρNW1(A,A)=1 ;\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| {{\rho _{NW1}}(A,B)} \right| \le 1$$\end{document}ρNW1(A,B)≤1 .

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\rho _{NW1}}(A,B) = {\rho _{NW1}}(B,A)$$\end{document}ρNW1(A,B)=ρNW1(B,A) ;

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\rho _{NW1}}(A,B) = {\rho _{NW1}}(B,A)$$\end{document}ρNW1(A,B)=ρNW1(B,A) ;

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\rho _{NW1}}(A,A) = 1$$\end{document}ρNW1(A,A)=1 ;

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\rho _{NW1}}(A,A) = 1$$\end{document}ρNW1(A,A)=1 ;

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| {{\rho _{NW1}}(A,B)} \right| \le 1$$\end{document}ρNW1(A,B)≤1 .

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| {{\rho _{NW1}}(A,B)} \right| \le 1$$\end{document}ρNW1(A,B)≤1 .

Proof

The conclusions of (1) and (1) are obvious, and we will not give the detailed proof process. As to (1), it can be proved as below:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\begin{array}{l} \left| {C(A,B)} \right| \\ = \left| {\frac{{\sum \limits _{i = 1}^n {\left[ {\left( {{{{\overline{h}} }_A}({x_i}) - {\overline{\psi }} ({h_A}({x_i}))} \right) \cdot \left( {{{{\overline{h}} }_B}({x_i}) - {\overline{\psi }} ({h_B}({x_i}))} \right) } \right] } }}{n}} \right| \\ \le \frac{{\sum \limits _{i = 1}^n {\left| {\left( {{{{\overline{h}} }_A}({x_i}) - {\overline{\psi }} ({h_A}({x_i}))} \right) } \right| \cdot \left| {\left( {{{{\overline{h}} }_B}({x_i}) - {\overline{\psi }} ({h_B}({x_i}))} \right) } \right| } }}{n}. \end{array}\end{aligned}$$\end{document}C(A,B)=∑i=1nh¯A(xi)-ψ¯(hA(xi))·h¯B(xi)-ψ¯(hB(xi))n≤∑i=1nh¯A(xi)-ψ¯(hA(xi))·h¯B(xi)-ψ¯(hB(xi))n.In light of Cauchy–Schwarz inequality is:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}{} & {} {\left( {{a_1}{b_1} + {a_2}{b_2} + \cdots + {a_n}{b_n}} \right) ^2}\\{} & {} \quad \le \left( {{a_1}^2 + {a_2}^2 + \cdots + {a_n}^2} \right) \cdot \left( {{b_1}^2 + {b_2}^2 + \cdots + {b_n}^2} \right) , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}a1b1+a2b2+⋯+anbn2≤a12+a22+⋯+an2·b12+b22+⋯+bn2,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_{i}, b_{i} \in R, i=1,2, \ldots , n$$\end{document}ai,bi∈R,i=1,2,…,n, thus\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left| {C(A,B)} \right|{} & {} \le {\left( \begin{array}{l} \frac{1}{n}\sum \limits _{i = 1}^n {{{\left| {\left( {{{{\overline{h}} }_A}({x_i}) - {\overline{\psi }} ({h_A}({x_i}))} \right) } \right| }^2}} \cdot \\ \frac{1}{n}\sum \limits _{i = 1}^n {{{\left| {\left( {{{{\overline{h}} }_B}({x_i}) - {\overline{\psi }} ({h_B}({x_i}))} \right) } \right| }^2}} \end{array} \right) ^{1/2}}\\{} & {} = {\left| {C(A,A)} \right| ^{1/2}} \cdot {\left| {C(B,B)} \right| ^{1/2}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}C(A,B)≤1n∑i=1nh¯A(xi)-ψ¯(hA(xi))2·1n∑i=1nh¯B(xi)-ψ¯(hB(xi))21/2=C(A,A)1/2·C(B,B)1/2.Then we can get the result:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\begin{array}{l} \left| {{\rho _{NW1}}(A,B)} \right| = \frac{{\left| {C(A,B)} \right| }}{{{{\left| {C(A,A)} \right| }^{1/2}} \cdot {{\left| {C(B,B)} \right| }^{1/2}}}} \le 1. \end{array}\end{aligned}$$\end{document}ρNW1(A,B)=C(A,B)C(A,A)1/2·C(B,B)1/2≤1.Subsequently, we extend the Hesitant Fuzzy Sets (HFSs) presented in Examples 1 and 2 to examine the efficacy of our defined correlation coefficient in revealing the relationships between variables. We recalculate these examples using the correlation coefficient developed for Normal Wiggly Hesitant Fuzzy Sets (NWHFSs) to validate its ability to expose the underlying connections.

Example 5

length, we get the correlation coefficients between them as following:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}{} & {} \begin{array}{l} {\rho _{NW1}}(A,B) = 0.8337, {\rho _{NW1}}(A,C) = 0.4623, \end{array} \\{} & {} \begin{array}{l} {\rho _{NW1}}(B,C) = 0.8751. \end{array} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ρNW1(A,B)=0.8337,ρNW1(A,C)=0.4623,ρNW1(B,C)=0.8751.

Our analysis reveals that the correlation coefficients we have developed are capable of addressing a unique case that remains unsolvable using the method proposed by26. This is achievable because our approach allows for the expression of more profound information through the incorporation of Normal Wiggly Elements (NWEs).

The distinctions and advantages of the correlation coefficient introduced in this paper are detailed as follows:Reference25 defined a scenario where the correlation coefficients between Hesitant Fuzzy Sets (HFSs) are confined to the interval [0,1]. This approach, however, does not align with traditional random variable analysis, which necessitates the depiction of negative values as well. Consequently, the correlation coefficients for Normal Wiggly Hesitant Fuzzy Sets (NWHFSs) should be situated within the interval [-1,1] to provide a more comprehensive representation.Reference25 stipulated that the number of values within two Hesitant Fuzzy Elements (HFEs) must be identical. In cases where they are not, the approach suggested either the application of a pessimistic or optimistic principle to augment the set with fewer values. However, the insertion of values into HFEs risks altering or misrepresenting the original information provided by decision-makers (DMs), potentially resulting in inaccurate decision outcomes. In this work, we have chosen to ease this restriction, eliminating the constraint on the length of Normal Wiggly Hesitant Fuzzy Elements (NWHFEs).Reference27 highlighted that26’s approach was primarily centered on the mean values of Hesitant Fuzzy Elements (HFEs), leading to definitions that were based solely on these means. In contrast,27 enhanced the correlation coefficients by incorporating both the length and distribution of HFEs, in addition to their mean values. However, both approaches failed to account for the subjective and implicit preferences of decision-makers (DMs). The correlation coefficient proposed for Normal Evidential Hesitant Fuzzy Sets (NEHFSs) in this paper is designed to assess the degree of alignment between two variables, taking into consideration both the explicit information and the latent preferences of DMs.Overall, the newly proposed correlation measurement approach is designed to bypass the inherent limitations of current correlation coefficients utilized for HFSs. Taking into account the pessimistic psychological tendencies of decision-makers (DMs), our method introduces a novel concept that maximizes the values of correlation within a set, thereby defining a unique correlation coefficient applicable to NWHFSs.

Reference25 defined a scenario where the correlation coefficients between Hesitant Fuzzy Sets (HFSs) are confined to the interval [0,1]. This approach, however, does not align with traditional random variable analysis, which necessitates the depiction of negative values as well. Consequently, the correlation coefficients for Normal Wiggly Hesitant Fuzzy Sets (NWHFSs) should be situated within the interval [-1,1] to provide a more comprehensive representation.

Reference25 defined a scenario where the correlation coefficients between Hesitant Fuzzy Sets (HFSs) are confined to the interval [0,1]. This approach, however, does not align with traditional random variable analysis, which necessitates the depiction of negative values as well. Consequently, the correlation coefficients for Normal Wiggly Hesitant Fuzzy Sets (NWHFSs) should be situated within the interval [-1,1] to provide a more comprehensive representation.

Reference25 stipulated that the number of values within two Hesitant Fuzzy Elements (HFEs) must be identical. In cases where they are not, the approach suggested either the application of a pessimistic or optimistic principle to augment the set with fewer values. However, the insertion of values into HFEs risks altering or misrepresenting the original information provided by decision-makers (DMs), potentially resulting in inaccurate decision outcomes. In this work, we have chosen to ease this restriction, eliminating the constraint on the length of Normal Wiggly Hesitant Fuzzy Elements (NWHFEs).

Reference25 stipulated that the number of values within two Hesitant Fuzzy Elements (HFEs) must be identical. In cases where they are not, the approach suggested either the application of a pessimistic or optimistic principle to augment the set with fewer values. However, the insertion of values into HFEs risks altering or misrepresenting the original information provided by decision-makers (DMs), potentially resulting in inaccurate decision outcomes. In this work, we have chosen to ease this restriction, eliminating the constraint on the length of Normal Wiggly Hesitant Fuzzy Elements (NWHFEs).

Reference27 highlighted that26’s approach was primarily centered on the mean values of Hesitant Fuzzy Elements (HFEs), leading to definitions that were based solely on these means. In contrast,27 enhanced the correlation coefficients by incorporating both the length and distribution of HFEs, in addition to their mean values. However, both approaches failed to account for the subjective and implicit preferences of decision-makers (DMs). The correlation coefficient proposed for Normal Evidential Hesitant Fuzzy Sets (NEHFSs) in this paper is designed to assess the degree of alignment between two variables, taking into consideration both the explicit information and the latent preferences of DMs.

Reference27 highlighted that26’s approach was primarily centered on the mean values of Hesitant Fuzzy Elements (HFEs), leading to definitions that were based solely on these means. In contrast,27 enhanced the correlation coefficients by incorporating both the length and distribution of HFEs, in addition to their mean values. However, both approaches failed to account for the subjective and implicit preferences of decision-makers (DMs). The correlation coefficient proposed for Normal Evidential Hesitant Fuzzy Sets (NEHFSs) in this paper is designed to assess the degree of alignment between two variables, taking into consideration both the explicit information and the latent preferences of DMs.

Theorem 2

Comparing the two correlation coefficients between NWHFSs introduced in Definitions 7 and 8, they satisfy the following property:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\begin{array}{l} \left| {{\rho _{NW2}}(A,B)} \right| \le \left| {{\rho _{NW1}}(A,B)} \right| . \end{array}\end{aligned}$$\end{document}ρNW2(A,B)≤ρNW1(A,B).

Proof

Since,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\begin{array}{l} C{(A,A)^{1/2}} \cdot C{(B,B)^{1/2}}\\ \le {\left( {\max \left\{ {{C^2}(A,A),{C^2}(B,B)} \right\} } \right) ^{1/2}}\\ = \max \left\{ {C(A,A),C(B,B)} \right\} \end{array}\end{aligned}$$\end{document}C(A,A)1/2·C(B,B)1/2≤maxC2(A,A),C2(B,B)1/2=maxC(A,A),C(B,B)Thus,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\begin{array}{l} \left| {{\rho _{NW2}}(A,B)} \right| \\ = \frac{{\left| {C(A,B)} \right| }}{{\max \left\{ {C(A,A),C(B,B)} \right\} }} \le \frac{{\left| {C(A,B)} \right| }}{{{{\left[ {C(A,A) \cdot C(B,B)} \right] }^{1/2}}}}\\ = \left| {{\rho _{NW1}}(A,B)} \right| . \end{array} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ρNW2(A,B)=C(A,B)maxC(A,A),C(B,B)≤C(A,B)C(A,A)·C(B,B)1/2=ρNW1(A,B).

Theorem 3

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\rho _{NW2}}(A,B) = {\rho _{NW2}}(B,A)$$\end{document}ρNW2(A,B)=ρNW2(B,A) ;\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\rho _{NW2}}(A,A) = 1$$\end{document}ρNW2(A,A)=1 ;\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| {{\rho _{NW2}}(A,B)} \right| \le 1$$\end{document}ρNW2(A,B)≤1 .

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\rho _{NW2}}(A,B) = {\rho _{NW2}}(B,A)$$\end{document}ρNW2(A,B)=ρNW2(B,A) ;

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\rho _{NW2}}(A,B) = {\rho _{NW2}}(B,A)$$\end{document}ρNW2(A,B)=ρNW2(B,A) ;

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\rho _{NW2}}(A,A) = 1$$\end{document}ρNW2(A,A)=1 ;

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\rho _{NW2}}(A,A) = 1$$\end{document}ρNW2(A,A)=1 ;

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| {{\rho _{NW2}}(A,B)} \right| \le 1$$\end{document}ρNW2(A,B)≤1 .

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| {{\rho _{NW2}}(A,B)} \right| \le 1$$\end{document}ρNW2(A,B)≤1 .

Proof

The proof process of properties (3) and (3) is similar to Theorem 1. As for (3), according to Theorem 2, we can get\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\begin{array}{l} \left| {{\rho _{NW2}}(A,B)} \right| \le \left| {{\rho _{NW1}}(A,B)} \right| , \end{array} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ρNW2(A,B)≤ρNW1(A,B),thus, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| \rho _{N W 2}(A, B)\right| \le \left| \rho _{N W 1}(A, B)\right| \le 1$$\end{document}ρNW2(A,B)≤ρNW1(A,B)≤1.

In realistic situation, objects \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_{i} \in X(i=1,2, \ldots , n)$$\end{document}xi∈X(i=1,2,…,n) may be in different degree of importance. DMs will assign different weights to them. Based on this assumption, we propose the weighted correlation coefficients between NWHFSs.

Definition 9

For a reference set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X=\left\{ x_{1}, x_{2}, \ldots ,\right.$$\end{document}X=x1,x2,…, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left. x_{n}\right\}$$\end{document}xn, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega =\left[ \omega _{1}, \omega _{2}, \ldots , \omega _{n}\right] ^{T}$$\end{document}ω=ω1,ω2,…,ωnT be a weighting vector of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_{i} \in X$$\end{document}xi∈X with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega _{i}>0,(i=1,2, \ldots , n)$$\end{document}ωi>0,(i=1,2,…,n) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _{i=1}^{n} \omega _{i}=1$$\end{document}∑i=1nωi=1. For any two NWHFSs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A=\left\{ \left\langle x, h_{A}\left( x_{i}\right) , \psi \left( h_{A}\left( x_{i}\right) \right) \right\rangle | x_{i}\right.$$\end{document}A=x,hAxi,ψhAxi|xi \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\in X\}$$\end{document}∈X} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B=\left\{ \left\langle x, h_{B}\left( x_{i}\right) , \psi \left( h_{B}\left( x_{i}\right) \right) \right\rangle | x_{i} \in X\right\}$$\end{document}B=x,hBxi,ψhBxi|xi∈X on X, the weighted correlation coefficients between NWHFSs A and B can be defined as following:17\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}{} & {} \begin{array}{l} {\rho _{NW\omega 1}}(A,B) = \frac{{{C_\omega }(A,B)}}{{{C_\omega }{{(A,A)}^{1/2}} \cdot {C_\omega }{{(B,B)}^{1/2}}}}\\ \quad = \frac{{\sum \limits _{i = 1}^n {{\omega _i}\left[ {\left( {{{{\overline{h}} }_A}({x_i}) - {\overline{\psi }} ({h_A}({x_i}))} \right) \cdot \left( {{{{\overline{h}} }_B}({x_i}) - {\overline{\psi }} ({h_B}({x_i}))} \right) } \right] } }}{{\sqrt{\sum \limits _{i = 1}^n {{\omega _i}{{\left[ {{{{\overline{h}} }_A}({x_i}) - {\overline{\psi }} ({h_A}({x_i}))} \right] }^2}} } \cdot \sqrt{\sum \limits _{i = 1}^n {{\omega _i}{{\left[ {{{{\overline{h}} }_B}({x_i}) - {\overline{\psi }} ({h_B}({x_i}))} \right] }^2}} } }}, \end{array} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ρNWω1(A,B)=Cω(A,B)Cω(A,A)1/2·Cω(B,B)1/2=∑i=1nωih¯A(xi)-ψ¯(hA(xi))·h¯B(xi)-ψ¯(hB(xi))∑i=1nωih¯A(xi)-ψ¯(hA(xi))2·∑i=1nωih¯B(xi)-ψ¯(hB(xi))2,18\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}{} & {} \begin{array}{l} {\rho _{NW\omega 2}}(A,B) = \frac{{{C_\omega }(A,B)}}{{\max \left\{ {{C_\omega }(A,A),{C_\omega }(B,B)} \right\} }}\\ \quad = \frac{{\sum \limits _{i = 1}^n {{\omega _i}\left[ {\left( {{{{\overline{h}} }_A}({x_i}) - {\overline{\psi }} ({h_A}({x_i}))} \right) \cdot \left( {{{{\overline{h}} }_B}({x_i}) - {\overline{\psi }} ({h_B}({x_i}))} \right) } \right] } }}{{\max \left\{ {\sum \limits _{i = 1}^n {{\omega _i}{{\left[ {{{{\overline{h}} }_A}({x_i}) - {\overline{\psi }} ({h_A}({x_i}))} \right] }^2}},\sum \limits _{i = 1}^n {{\omega _i}{{\left[ {{{{\overline{h}} }_B}({x_i}) - {\overline{\psi }} ({h_B}({x_i}))} \right] }^2}} } \right\} }}. \end{array} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ρNWω2(A,B)=Cω(A,B)maxCω(A,A),Cω(B,B)=∑i=1nωih¯A(xi)-ψ¯(hA(xi))·h¯B(xi)-ψ¯(hB(xi))max∑i=1nωih¯A(xi)-ψ¯(hA(xi))2,∑i=1nωih¯B(xi)-ψ¯(hB(xi))2.

We can prove the weighted correlation coefficients \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho _{N W \omega 1}(A, B)$$\end{document}ρNWω1(A,B) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho _{N W \omega 2}(A, B)$$\end{document}ρNWω2(A,B) both satisfy the three properties as shown in Theorem 1.

It is evident that the correlation coefficients derived from Eqs. (15) and (17) employ the geometric mean of the correlations within NWHFSs. In contrast, Eqs. (16) and (18) prioritize the maximum value of these correlations. Consequently, when DMs exhibit an optimistic perspective, the correlation coefficients formulated in Eqs. (15) and (17) are well-suited for both non-weighted and weighted criteria environments, respectively. On the other hand, for DMs with a pessimistic outlook, Eqs. (16) and (18) effectively address the needs of non-weighted and weighted criteria information, respectively.

In the subsequent section, we delve into various applications by harnessing the novel correlation coefficients introduced in this study.

Typical applications and comparisons

In this section, we validate our approach by comparing it with existing correlation coefficients through two illustrative applications: the Multi-Criteria Decision-Making (MCDM) process and clustering analysis.The data used in this section comes from our market research and questionnaires, and the integration of these different data streams has been executed with precision to ensure that our analysis is not only based on empirical evidence, but also echoes actual reality.

MCDM approach based on correlation coefficients between NWHFSs

In this subsection, we will illustrate MCDM process and its practical example with the extended correlation coefficients. There are five steps in the MCDM process:

Step1: Suppose that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R=\left\{ R_{1}, R_{2}, \ldots , R_{n}\right\}$$\end{document}R=R1,R2,…,Rn is an alternatives set and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C=\left\{ C_{1}, C_{2}, \ldots , C_{m}\right\}$$\end{document}C=C1,C2,…,Cm is a criteria set. Then, let DMs provide their evaluation data as the HFEs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_{i j}(i=1,2, \ldots , n ; j=1,2, \ldots , m)$$\end{document}hij(i=1,2,…,n;j=1,2,…,m) for the alternatives under criteria. Then the HFEs constitute a hesitant fuzzy decision matrix H, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H=\left[ h_{i j}\right]$$\end{document}H=hij.

Step2: Excavating the potential preference degrees of DMs from HFEs by Definition 4. Afterwards, combining it with original information to form NWHFEs: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_{i j}=\left\{ h_{i j}(x), \psi \left( h_{i j}(x)\right) \right\}$$\end{document}nij=hij(x),ψhij(x). Then constructing normal wiggly hesitant fuzzy decision matrix: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_{i j}=\left[ n_{i j}\right]$$\end{document}Nij=nij.

Step3: DMs provide the criteria weighting vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega =\left[ \omega _{1}, \omega _{2}, \ldots , \omega _{n}\right] ^{T}$$\end{document}ω=ω1,ω2,…,ωnT and reference set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^{*}$$\end{document}R∗ according to realistic situation. Then, transforming the original reference set into NWHFS reference set.

Step4: Calculating the weighted correlation coefficient between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_{i}(i=1,2, \ldots , n)$$\end{document}Ri(i=1,2,…,n) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^{*}$$\end{document}R∗ by utilizing Eq. (17) or Eq. (18).

Step5: Ranking the alternatives and choosing the best one among them with respect to the weighted correlation coefficient.

Example 6

Suppose a communications company is seeking to hire a salesperson. Following a written examination, four candidates have been shortlisted for an interview:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_{1}$$\end{document}R1, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_{2}$$\end{document}R2, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_{3}$$\end{document}R3 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_{4}$$\end{document}R4. The interviewers are tasked with selecting the most suitable candidate from this pool. Their decision will be based on four criteria: professional knowledge(\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{1}$$\end{document}C1), verbal ability (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{2}$$\end{document}C2), team cooperation spirit (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{3}$$\end{document}C3) and analytical skills (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{4}$$\end{document}C4). These criteria are designed to complement the information that may be insufficiently captured in the written examination.

Step1:Interviewers are requested to assess the candidates based on the four established criteria and subsequently provide their evaluations in the form of an original hesitant fuzzy decision matrix, as presented in Table 2.

Step2: The original HFEs are automatically transformed into NWHFEs in accordance with Definition 4. The resulting normal wiggly hesitant fuzzy decision matrix is displayed in Table 3.

Step3: Assuming that no ideal candidate emerges from the interview process, interviewers, in their pursuit to select the most suitable salesman, assign a weighting vector to the criteria, denoted as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega =[0.20,0.32,0.27,0.21]^{T}$$\end{document}ω=[0.20,0.32,0.27,0.21]T. Additionally, the original reference set of candidate evaluations is established as follows:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\begin{array}{l} {R^*} = \left\{ \begin{array}{l} \left\langle {{C_1},(0.7,0.8)} \right\rangle ,\left\langle {{C_2},(0.7,0.8,0.9)} \right\rangle ,\\ \left\langle {{C_3},(0.7,0.9)} \right\rangle ,\left\langle {{C_4},(0.8,0.9)} \right\rangle \end{array} \right\} . \end{array} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}R∗=C1,(0.7,0.8),C2,(0.7,0.8,0.9),C3,(0.7,0.9),C4,(0.8,0.9).Then, the NWHFS reference candidate set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N W_{R^{*}}$$\end{document}NWR∗ can be obtained by Definition 4 as below:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} N{W_{{R^*}}}= \left\{ \begin{array}{l} \left\langle \begin{array}{l} {C_1},(0.7,0.8),(0.6697,0.7020,0.7303),\\ (0.7697,0.8020,0.8303) \end{array} \right\rangle ,\\ \left\langle \begin{array}{l} {C_2},(0.7,0.8,0.9),(0.6614,0.7032,0.7386),\\ (0.7184,0.8068,0.8816),\\ (0.8614,0.9032,0.9386) \end{array} \right\rangle ,\\ \left\langle \begin{array}{l} {C_3},(0.7,0.9),(0.6393,0.7076,0.7607),\\ (0.8393,0.9076,0.9607) \end{array} \right\rangle ,\\ \left\langle \begin{array}{l} {C_4},(0.8,0.9),(0.7697,0.8018,0.8303),\\ (0.8697,0.9018,0.9303) \end{array} \right\rangle \end{array} \right\} . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}NWR∗=C1,(0.7,0.8),(0.6697,0.7020,0.7303),(0.7697,0.8020,0.8303),C2,(0.7,0.8,0.9),(0.6614,0.7032,0.7386),(0.7184,0.8068,0.8816),(0.8614,0.9032,0.9386),C3,(0.7,0.9),(0.6393,0.7076,0.7607),(0.8393,0.9076,0.9607),C4,(0.8,0.9),(0.7697,0.8018,0.8303),(0.8697,0.9018,0.9303).Step4: The weighted correlation coefficients between candidates and NWHFS reference candidate set can be calculated by Eq. (17):\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}{} & {} \begin{array}{l} \rho _{ N W \omega {1}}\left( R_{1}, R^{*}\right) =0.8125; \end{array}\\{} & {} \begin{array}{l} \rho _{ N W \omega {1}}\left( R_{2}, R^{*}\right) =0.0125; \end{array}\\{} & {} \begin{array}{l} \rho _{ N W \omega {1}}\left( R_{3}, R^{*}\right) =-0.7013; \end{array} \\{} & {} \begin{array}{l} \rho _{ N W \omega {1}}\left( R_{4}, R^{*}\right) =0.3660. \end{array} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ρNWω1R1,R∗=0.8125;ρNWω1R2,R∗=0.0125;ρNWω1R3,R∗=-0.7013;ρNWω1R4,R∗=0.3660.Step5: Based on the weighted correlation coefficients, the ranking order of the four candidates is determined to be \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_{1} \succ R_{4} \succ R_{2} \succ R_{3}$$\end{document}R1≻R4≻R2≻R3. Consequently, the first candidate is recommended for recruitment as a salesman for the communications company.

For comparative purposes, we employ the correlation coefficient formulas proposed by25 and26to rank the candidates. It should be noted that in the case of25, a pessimistic principle is applied to normalize the original HFSs information25. The correlation coefficients between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_{i}$$\end{document}Ri and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^{*}$$\end{document}R∗ are calculated using Eq. (2). Furthermore, the correlation coefficients between the candidates and the NWHFS reference candidate set are derived using Eq. (5). These results are presented in Table 4 and Fig. 2.
