Figure 3Comparing the correlation coefficient values of different methodologies (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho _{N W \omega 2}\left( R_{i}, R^{*}\right)$$\end{document}ρNWω2Ri,R∗).

Comparing the correlation coefficient values of different methodologies (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho _{N W \omega 1}\left( R_{i}, R^{*}\right)$$\end{document}ρNWω1Ri,R∗).

Comparing the correlation coefficient values of different methodologies (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho _{N W \omega 2}\left( R_{i}, R^{*}\right)$$\end{document}ρNWω2Ri,R∗).

Based on the correlation formula presented by25, the ranking outcome calculated using Eq. (2) is found to be largely consistent with the approach of this paper. Our analysis reveals that candidate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_{1}$$\end{document}R1 is identified as the most suitable among all contenders. In contrast, candidate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_{4}$$\end{document}R4 is ranked fourth, which does not account for the potential preferences of the DMs. When employing the correlation coefficients proposed in this paper, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_{4}$$\end{document}R4 is considered a less optimal candidate. The primary reason for this divergence is the incorporation of NWEs in our calculation of the correlation coefficients, thereby integrating valuable, implicit information from the DMs. Furthermore, the range of correlation coefficient values derived from Eq. (2)—from 0.948 to 0.967—is too narrow to effectively distinguish between the candidates. While all correlation coefficient values proposed by25 are positive,26 has addressed this limitation. However, the candidate rankings yielded by Eq. (5) are in direct opposition to those from Eq. (2). It is not a straightforward task to ascertain which result is superior. Nevertheless, in the recruitment process, the subjective impressions of the interviewers often carry significant weight in determining the final outcome. Beyond the raw data, it is essential to consider the unexpressed potential preferences that may come to light during interviews. We assert that a more compelling case is made by taking both the explicit and implicit aspects into account.

To further underscore the merits of our proposed methodology, especially in scenarios where DMs exhibit a pessimistic outlook, we have chosen \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho _{N W \omega 2}\left( R_{i}, R^{*}\right)$$\end{document}ρNWω2Ri,R∗ as a benchmark for comparison. Both25 and26 have developed correlation coefficients predicated on the maximum values of these correlations. A comparison with the ranking outcomes yielded by Eq. (18) is depicted in Fig. 3. It becomes evident that the rankings align with those presented in Fig. 2. However, the range of correlation coefficient values as determined by26 is somewhat constricted, whereas the correlation metrics derived from Eq. (18) maintain distinct differences. These discernible differences are beneficial in facilitating DMs to more readily identify the most suitable candidate.

Clustering analysis based on the correlation coefficients between NWHFSs

Clustering analysis, a pivotal technique within the realm of data mining, enables the categorization of data sources that exhibit common characteristics. This analytical tool holds significant utility across various domains, including statistics40 and software evaluation41. In the following subsection, we introduce an algorithm for clustering analysis along with a practical example that leverages the correlation coefficients between NWHFSs.

Step1: Suppose that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X=\left\{ x_{1}, x_{2}, \ldots , x_{m}\right\}$$\end{document}X=x1,x2,…,xm is a reference set, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B=\left\{ B_{1}, B_{2}, \ldots , B_{n}\right\}$$\end{document}B=B1,B2,…,Bn is a set of HFSs on X. Then, we transform the original HFSs into NWHFSs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D=\left\{ D_{1}, D_{2}, \ldots , D_{n}\right\}$$\end{document}D=D1,D2,…,Dn by Definition 4.

Step2: DMs give the criteria weighting vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega =\left[ \omega _{1}, \omega _{2}, \ldots , \omega _{n}\right] ^{T}$$\end{document}ω=ω1,ω2,…,ωnT. Then, measuring the correlation coefficients based on Eq. (17) between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_{i}$$\end{document}Di and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_{j}(i, j$$\end{document}Dj(i,j \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$=1,2, \ldots , n)$$\end{document}=1,2,…,n). Afterwards, according to the correlation coefficients \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho _{i j}=\rho _{ N W \omega {1}}\left( D_{i}, D_{j}\right)$$\end{document}ρij=ρNWω1Di,Dj, we construct a correlation matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C=\left( \rho _{i j}\right) _{n \times n}$$\end{document}C=ρijn×n.

Step3: If the matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C=\left( \rho _{i j}\right) _{n \times n}$$\end{document}C=ρijn×n satisfies the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^{2} \subseteq C$$\end{document}C2⊆C, i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^{2}=C \circ C=\left( \tilde{\rho }_{i j}\right) _{n \times n}$$\end{document}C2=C∘C=ρ~ijn×n, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\tilde{\rho }} _{ij}} = \mathop {\max }\limits _k \left\{ {\min } \right. \{ {\rho _{ik}},$$\end{document}ρ~ij=maxkmin{ρik, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left. \left. \rho _{k j}\right\} \right\}$$\end{document}ρkj, we can call C as equivalent correlation matrix. Otherwise, we should convert the original correlation matrix into equivalent correlation matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^{2^{k}}$$\end{document}Ck: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C \rightarrow C^{2} \rightarrow \ldots C^{2^{k}} \rightarrow \ldots$$\end{document}C→C2→…Ck→… until \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^{2^{k}}=C^{2^{k+1}}$$\end{document}Ck=Ck+1.

Step4: Suppose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \in [0,1]$$\end{document}λ∈[0,1] be a confidence level. We could construct a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda$$\end{document}λ-cut matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{\lambda }=\left( \lambda \rho _{i j}\right) _{n \times n}$$\end{document}Cλ=λρijn×n:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\begin{array}{l} \lambda {\rho _{ij}} = \left\{ {\begin{array}{*{20}{l}} {0\quad \mathrm{{ if }}\quad {\rho _{ij}} < \lambda }\\ {1\quad \mathrm{{ if }}\quad {\rho _{ij}} \ge \lambda } \end{array}} \right. \end{array}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}λρij=0ifρij<λ1ifρij≥λ.If the values in ith line or column are the same as the corresponding values in jth line or column of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{\lambda }$$\end{document}Cλ, we can conclude that NWHFSs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_{i}$$\end{document}Di and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_{j}$$\end{document}Dj are the same type.

Example 7

Currently, a company may engage with several upstream suppliers, whose service quality varies. Consequently, it becomes necessary to categorize these potential suppliers based on specific criteria: enterprise performance (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X_{1}$$\end{document}X1), product quality (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X_{2}$$\end{document}X2), product price (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X_{3}$$\end{document}X3), and after-sales service quality (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X_{4}$$\end{document}X4).

Step1: DMs assess seven suppliers against these four criteria, initially using HFS. Subsequently, we convert these HFSs into NWHFSs to better leverage the embedded, or concealed, information. The resulting NWHFS decision matrix for the seven suppliers, denoted as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_{i}(i=1,2, \ldots , 7)$$\end{document}Di(i=1,2,…,7) across the four criteria is presented in Table 5.

The clustering result of seven suppliers by our method.

When compared with the clustering analysis algorithms put forward by25 and26, we obtain distinct classification outcomes as illustrated in Tables 7 and 8, respectively. It becomes evident that26 remedies a limitation present in25’s approach, where the confidence levels are excessively restrictive for differentiating among various suppliers.26’s method places significant emphasis on the mean values of HFEs to formulate the correlation coefficients. Reliance on a single characteristic could potentially result in inaccurate classification outcomes. In contrast, our methodology takes into account both the original information and the preference degrees implied by DMs, offering a more nuanced reflection.

Through the aforementioned examples, we have substantiated the merits of the novel correlation coefficients associated with NWHFS when applied within the context of MCDM and clustering analysis. The newly proposed correlation coefficients have the capacity to expand the spectrum of decision values and confidence levels. This expansion is instrumental in the classification of diverse alternatives. Given that NWHFS can encapsulate evaluation information in a more holistic manner, it subsequently enhances the precision of the outcomes. However, when confronted with substantial volumes of data, the computational process utilizing the proposed correlation coefficients may not be sufficiently succinct. Therefore, we intend to explore extended forms of NWHFS in future research endeavors.Table 7The clustering result of seven suppliers by25.ClassConfidence levelClusters7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} Conclusion

Our study presents a novel methodology that integrates Normal Wiggly Hesitant Fuzzy Sets (NWHFSs) and their correlation coefficients, offering a refined approach to multi-criteria decision-making (MCDM) and clustering analysis. The proposed methodology excels in capturing the subtleties of decision-makers’ preferences, including both explicit evaluations and implicit, nuanced inclinations. This advancement addresses the limitations inherent in traditional Hesitant Fuzzy Sets (HFS), which often fail to encapsulate the full spectrum of decision-makers’ preferences25,26.

The advantages of our approach are threefold: Firstly, the correlation coefficients developed for NWHFSs provide a more comprehensive representation of the relationships between variables, allowing for negative values and thus adhering to traditional statistical principles. Secondly, our methodology removes the constraint of equal length for Hesitant Fuzzy Elements (HFEs), mitigating the risk of misrepresenting decision-makers’ original information. Additionally, the weighted correlation coefficients cater to scenarios where decision-makers exhibit either optimistic or pessimistic outlooks, enhancing the applicability of our method in diverse decision-making contexts.

However, our methodology is not without limitations. The computational process may become cumbersome when dealing with large datasets, and the complexity of NWHFSs could potentially overshadow the decision content itself. Future research will focus on extending the NWHFS framework to accommodate these challenges, ensuring both the scalability and accessibility of our approach.

In conclusion, the integration of NWHFS and their correlation coefficients presents a significant advancement in the field of decision-making, offering a robust, insightful, and nuanced framework. Our methodology not only redefines the MCDM paradigm but also provides a novel lens for addressing the complexities of decision-making in real-world scenarios.
